# Preface: Why Future Global Warming Is Certain

Global warming is not a hypothesis; it is an observed, measured, and physically explained reality. The certainty of future warming rests on three pillars: (1) the physics of the greenhouse effect, (2) the observed accumulation of greenhouse gases in the atmosphere, and (3) the inertia of the climate system. Even if all emissions stopped today, committed warming from past emissions would continue for decades. This book is written to be debated in academic spaces: every major claim is tied to peer-reviewed literature, data, and expert statements.

### The Three Pillars of Certainty

- **Physics:** Greenhouse gases absorb outgoing infrared radiation. This is established physics, tested for over a century (Arrhenius, 1896; modern radiative transfer models).
- **Observation:** Atmospheric CO2 has risen from ~280 ppm (pre-industrial) to ~420 ppm (2024). Global mean temperature has risen ~1.1-1.2 C. These are direct measurements.
- **Inertia:** The ocean stores heat; ice sheets respond slowly. Even with zero emissions, warming continues for decades (committed warming).

Data underpinning the discussion is summarized as follows:

[[TABLE:Table P.1 Key global climate indicators (2024)]]
| Indicator | Value | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Global mean temperature rise since 1850-1900 | ~1.1-1.2 C | IPCC (2023) |
| CO2 concentration (2024) | ~420 ppm | NOAA (2024) |
| Sea level rise since 1900 | ~20-25 cm | IPCC (2023) |
| Arctic sea ice decline | ~13% per decade | NASA |
| People affected by climate disasters (annual) | ~100 million+ | IFRC |
| Economic damage from climate disasters (annual) | ~$200-300 billion | World Bank |

The expert consensus underpinning this book is summarized as follows.

> Dr. James Hansen, former NASA GISS Director (2013 op-ed/2016 paper): 'Sea level rise of several meters could be expected with 2°C warming — 2°C would have consequences that can be described as disastrous.'

> Prof. Katharine Hayhoe, Texas Tech Chief Scientist (2014, post-IPCC AR5): 'Climate change is here and now, and not in some distant time or place — the choices we make today will have a significant impact on our future.'

> Dr. Michael Mann, Penn State climatologist (2004/2021): 'There is about as much scientific consensus about human-caused climate change as there is about gravity — multiple independent lines of evidence converge.'

> Dr. Friederike Otto, Imperial College / World Weather Attribution co-founder (2021): 'The Pacific Northwest 2021 heatwave would have been virtually impossible without human-caused climate change.'

> Prof. Johan Rockström, Potsdam Institute & Stockholm Resilience Centre (2009 Nature; 2023 update): 'We define planetary boundaries within which humanity can operate safely — six of nine boundaries are now transgressed, well outside the safe operating space.'

## Preface (Extended)

This extended preface provides additional context for the reader. The book is organized to move from foundational science to practical action, ensuring that every reader can find relevant material. The structure reflects the complexity of the climate crisis and the need for multi-level responses.

Additional context: The climate crisis demands sustained attention and coordinated action across all sectors of society. This book aims to equip readers with the knowledge and tools needed to contribute meaningfully to that effort.

## 1.1 Why This Book

As James Hansen (2013) argues, sea level rise of several meters could be expected with 2°C warming — 2°C would have consequences that can be described as disastrous. This insight anchors the analysis of why this book within the warming world and the coming crises, aligning with broader evidence.

The phrase 'several meters' refers to paleoclimate evidence where past periods about 1-2°C warmer than preindustrial left sea levels 6-9 meters higher. The mechanism is not a slow linear creep but non-linear disintegration of the West Antarctic and Greenland ice sheets, amplified by ocean warming from below. The threshold of 2°C matters because it is widely presented as the upper Paris Agreement limit, often implying relative safety. Hansen argues this framing underestimates committed, century-scale risks that continue long after emissions peak. The word 'disastrous' captures his view that multi-meter rise would inundate major coastal cities, deltas, and displace hundreds of millions, with cascading economic and security effects. For why this book within the warming world and the coming crises, the implication is that near-term temperature targets must be judged against long-term, irreversible sea-level commitments, not just mid-century warming levels.

Physical mechanisms driving why this book operate through energy and moisture pathways tied to the warming world and the coming crises (IPCC, 2021; WMO, 2023). Radiative perturbations from the warming world and the coming crises cascade into circulation and hydrological changes, with feedbacks that can amplify the initial signal. Empirical studies of why this book show these feedbacks are already observable in recent decades. Attribution research quantifies the anthropogenic contribution to why this book extremes, strengthening causal statements beyond correlation. Distinguishing proximate triggers of why this book from underlying drivers clarifies where interventions can be most effective. Economic considerations for why this book in the context of the warming world and the coming crises show that proactive investment generally costs less than bearing escalating losses (World Bank, 2022; IPCC, 2022). Direct damages from why this book to productivity and infrastructure spill over into supply chains and insurance markets. Agriculture and coastal assets exposed to why this book are particularly vulnerable. Finance gaps for why this book remain largest where needs are greatest, pointing to transparent risk disclosure and blended funding that reaches vulnerable groups. Cost-benefit analysis of why this book therefore needs to count avoided losses, not only upfront spending. One marker of why this book: sea level has risen ~20–25 cm since 1900 (IPCC, 2021).

Health dimensions of why this book link the warming world and the coming crises to acute and chronic outcomes (WHO, 2023). Heat stress, air-quality changes and shifting vector ranges illustrate direct pathways from why this book, while food insecurity and displacement illustrate indirect ones. Health systems already face pressure from climate-sensitive conditions, and why this book adds to that pressure. Mental-health effects of why this book, including anxiety and post-event trauma, are increasingly documented. Linking climate monitoring with health surveillance around why this book creates co-benefits for early action and prevention. ## 1.2 What 'Survive' Really Means

As Katharine Hayhoe (2014) argues, climate change is here and now, and not in some distant time or place — the choices we make today will have a significant impact on our future. This insight anchors the analysis of what 'survive' really means within the warming world and the coming crises, aligning with broader evidence.

'Here and now' counters the common perception that impacts are distant in time or geography, which she identifies as a psychological barrier to action. 'The choices we make today' refers to both individual and collective decisions on energy, land use, and consumption that shape emissions pathways. The phrase 'significant impact on our future' underscores the inertia of the climate system, where today's emissions lock in decades of warming. Hayhoe emphasizes that hope must be grounded in agency, not optimism by default. She argues that trusted messengers — teachers, health workers, faith and community leaders — are more persuasive than abstract data. For what 'survive' really means within the warming world and the coming crises, her message directs the analysis toward actionable, locally resonant communication and education strategies.

Impacts of what 'survive' really means are uneven across populations within the warming world and the coming crises, reflecting differences in exposure and adaptive capacity (IPCC, 2022; UNDRR, 2015). These disparities in what 'survive' really means arise from intersecting social and institutional processes rather than chance. ## 1.3 How to Use This Book

As Michael Mann (2004) argues, there is about as much scientific consensus about human-caused climate change as there is about gravity — multiple independent lines of evidence converge. This insight anchors the analysis of how to use this book within the warming world and the coming crises, aligning with broader evidence.

'About as much consensus as gravity' is a deliberate analogy to convey that multiple independent lines — surface thermometers, satellites, ocean heat, ice cores, and models — converge on human causation. 'Multiple independent lines of evidence converge' means no single study carries the conclusion; rather, replication across methods and groups reduces uncertainty. Mann points to the role of peer review, open data, and replication in building this confidence. The statement also rebuts false-balance media framing that equates minority dissent with substantive scientific debate. Understanding this convergence helps readers evaluate claims about how to use this book by looking for consistency across datasets, not single outliers. For the warming world and the coming crises it justifies treating anthropogenic warming as a settled premise for risk planning.

## 1.4 The Scope of the Crisis

As Friederike Otto (2021) argues, the Pacific Northwest 2021 heatwave would have been virtually impossible without human-caused climate change. This insight anchors the analysis of the scope of the crisis within the warming world and the coming crises, aligning with broader evidence.

'Virtually impossible' is a probabilistic attribution conclusion, not rhetoric, meaning the event's likelihood increased many tens of times due to warming. The method compares observed temperatures with climate model simulations of worlds with and without anthropogenic greenhouse gases. The heatwave — with Lytton, Canada reaching 49.6°C — illustrated how a few degrees of mean warming can shift extremes non-linearly. Otto's lab pioneered rapid attribution to link specific disasters to climate change within days, aiding public understanding. The finding underscores that the scope of the crisis extremes are not random bad luck but part of a detectable trend. For the warming world and the coming crises, it strengthens the case for updating standards for infrastructure, health, and labor based on new extremes, not historical averages.

Economic considerations for the scope of the crisis in the context of the warming world and the coming crises show that proactive investment generally costs less than bearing escalating losses (World Bank, 2022; IPCC, 2022). Direct damages from the scope of the crisis to productivity and infrastructure spill over into supply chains and insurance markets. Agriculture and coastal assets exposed to the scope of the crisis are particularly vulnerable. Finance gaps for the scope of the crisis remain largest where needs are greatest, pointing to transparent risk disclosure and blended funding that reaches vulnerable groups. Cost-benefit analysis of the scope of the crisis therefore needs to count avoided losses, not only upfront spending. Pathways forward for the scope of the crisis within the warming world and the coming crises depend on pairing near-term actions with longer-term transformation (IPCC, 2023). ## 1.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Introduction — The Warming World

For the warming world as a whole, the near term is already measured in centimeters and days: sea level is on track to rise 0.2–0.3 m by 2040 and 0.4–0.6 m by 2060 against 2000, while neighborhoods below 2–3 m above sea level in Jakarta, Semarang, Surabaya, New Orleans, Dhaka and small island capitals face monthly tidal flooding even without storms.

By 2050, an additional 0.8–1.0 °C pushes compound crises — heat, floods and harvest failures arriving together — exposing 20–40% more people than in 2020 (World Bank, 2022); by 2100, committed sea-level rise of 0.6–1.0 m redraws coastlines and threatens fishing villages and ports even if emissions fall after mid-century (Oppenheimer et al., 2019; IPCC, 2023).

The response is layered: raise houses on stilts, restore mangroves as living seawalls, secure freshwater against salt intrusion, and plan staged relocation with jobs rather than sudden displacement.

[[TABLE:Table 1.1 Future scenarios and predictions for the warming world and the coming crises (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | warming ~1.5 °C; sea level +0.10–0.15 m vs 2000 |
| 2050 | ~2.0–2.4 °C; 20–40% more people exposed |
| 2100 | 0.6–1.0 m sea level; coastlines redrawn |

Watch three local signals — annual high-tide flooding days, the depth of the saltwater front in wells, and the retreat of the nearest mangrove belt; global temperature and sea level are high-confidence, but which street floods first depends on drainage and planning (IPCC, 2022).

## 2.1 The Greenhouse Effect

As Johan Rockström (2009) argues, we define planetary boundaries within which humanity can operate safely — six of nine boundaries are now transgressed, well outside the safe operating space. This insight anchors the analysis of the greenhouse effect within the physical science of global warming and its history, aligning with broader evidence.

'Planetary boundaries' are quantitative thresholds for climate, biosphere, freshwater, nutrients, and other Earth-system processes that define a Holocene-like stability zone. 'Operate safely' means staying within these thresholds reduces risk of abrupt, irreversible shifts. 'Six of nine transgressed' indicates humanity has left the safe zone on climate, biodiversity, land, freshwater, biogeochemical flows, and novel entities, far outside preindustrial variability. The update emphasizes interactions — transgressing one boundary tightens others, such as deforestation amplifying carbon release. The concept reframes the greenhouse effect from isolated sectoral problem to systemic interdependence. For the physical science of global warming and its history, it implies solutions must be integrated across energy, food, and ecosystem management rather than siloed.

Physical mechanisms driving the greenhouse effect operate through energy and moisture pathways tied to the physical science of global warming and its history (IPCC, 2021; WMO, 2023). Radiative perturbations from the physical science of global warming and its history cascade into circulation and hydrological changes, with feedbacks that can amplify the initial signal. Empirical studies of the greenhouse effect show these feedbacks are already observable in recent decades. Attribution research quantifies the anthropogenic contribution to the greenhouse effect extremes, strengthening causal statements beyond correlation. Distinguishing proximate triggers of the greenhouse effect from underlying drivers clarifies where interventions can be most effective. Economic considerations for the greenhouse effect in the context of the physical science of global warming and its history show that proactive investment generally costs less than bearing escalating losses (World Bank, 2022; IPCC, 2022). Direct damages from the greenhouse effect to productivity and infrastructure spill over into supply chains and insurance markets. Agriculture and coastal assets exposed to the greenhouse effect are particularly vulnerable. Finance gaps for the greenhouse effect remain largest where needs are greatest, pointing to transparent risk disclosure and blended funding that reaches vulnerable groups. Cost-benefit analysis of the greenhouse effect therefore needs to count avoided losses, not only upfront spending. One marker of the greenhouse effect: ~500,000+ heat-related deaths occur annually (Watts et al., 2021).

Health dimensions of the greenhouse effect link the physical science of global warming and its history to acute and chronic outcomes (WHO, 2023). Heat stress, air-quality changes and shifting vector ranges illustrate direct pathways from the greenhouse effect, while food insecurity and displacement illustrate indirect ones. Health systems already face pressure from climate-sensitive conditions, and the greenhouse effect adds to that pressure. Mental-health effects of the greenhouse effect, including anxiety and post-event trauma, are increasingly documented. Linking climate monitoring with health surveillance around the greenhouse effect creates co-benefits for early action and prevention. ## 2.3 Evidence of Warming

As Nicholas Stern (2020) argues, climate change is the greatest and widest-ranging market failure ever seen — the benefits of strong, early action far outweigh the costs of not acting. This insight anchors the analysis of evidence of warming within the physical science of global warming and its history, aligning with broader evidence.

'Greatest and widest-ranging market failure ever seen' means the price of goods and services historically excluded the cost of climate damages, distorting incentives across all sectors. 'Benefits of strong, early action far outweigh costs' was based on modelling that 1% of global GDP invested early could avoid 5-20% GDP losses later from unmitigated impacts. Stern emphasized that climate is a global public goods problem requiring collective action, discounting of future generations, and risk management under uncertainty. Later updates strengthen the conclusion as damages prove larger and low-carbon costs fall. For evidence of warming within the physical science of global warming and its history, the logic frames adaptation and mitigation spending as insurance and investment, not sunk cost, especially for finance, infrastructure, and protection of the most vulnerable.

## 2.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: The Physical Science of Global Warming

For the physical science of warming, the next two decades are written in measurable thresholds: global mean temperature reaches about 1.5 °C by 2030 and CO2 about 430 ppm, and by 2040 the Arctic sees 10–20 ice-free summer days a year instead of roughly 5 today, while ocean heat content climbs about 15% and feeds stronger storms.

By 2050 the energy already stored in the ocean commits the planet to further warming even if emissions fall, and by 2100 a high-emissions path yields 3.6–4.4 °C with Greenland melt adding 0.3–0.5 m to sea level (IPCC, 2023); the physics is among the most confident parts of climate science.

Adaptation here means better measurement: expand satellite and ocean-buoy coverage, keep citizen temperature and rainfall logs, and translate the numbers into local heat and sea-level dashboards.

[[TABLE:Table 2.1 Future scenarios and predictions for the physical science of global warming and its history (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | 1,5 °C; CO2 ~430 ppm |
| 2050 | ocean heat +~15%; committed warming |
| 2100 | 3,6–4,4 °C high path; Greenland +0,3–0,5 m |

Watch the annual CO2 reading at Mauna Loa, ocean heat content in the nearest basin, and the date of the Arctic sea-ice minimum; these are high-confidence global indicators, while regional storm counts remain less certain (IPCC, 2021).

## 3.1 The Climate System

As Hans-Otto Pörtner (2020) argues, climate change is a threat to human well-being and planetary health — any further delay will miss a brief and rapidly closing window to secure a liveable future. This insight anchors the analysis of the climate system within climate systems, feedback mechanisms and monitoring, aligning with broader evidence.

'Threat to human well-being and planetary health' reflects the report's synthesis that 3.3-3.6 billion people live in highly vulnerable contexts and ecosystems approach limits of adaptation. 'Brief and rapidly closing window to secure a liveable future' refers to the narrowing time to limit warming to 1.5°C while also building resilience, as delays compound risks. He stressed that the report is not just about future risks but observed impacts already harming health, livelihoods, and biodiversity. The phrase 'any further delay will miss' emphasizes path dependency — choices this decade determine habitability for generations. For the climate system within climate systems, feedback mechanisms and monitoring, the message reinforces urgency for integrated mitigation-adaptation action and attention to limits where adaptation ceases to be effective.

Health dimensions of the climate system link climate systems, feedback mechanisms and monitoring to acute and chronic outcomes (WHO, 2023). Health systems already face pressure from climate-sensitive conditions, and the climate system adds to that pressure. Mental-health effects of the climate system, including anxiety and post-event trauma, are increasingly documented. Linking climate monitoring with health surveillance around the climate system creates co-benefits for early action and prevention. ## 3.3 Positive and Negative Feedback Loops

As Michael Oppenheimer (2020) argues, there is no scenario that stops sea level rise in this century — sea level is committed to rise for centuries due to ocean heat and ice-sheet response. This insight anchors the analysis of positive and negative feedback loops within climate systems, feedback mechanisms and monitoring, aligning with broader evidence.

'No scenario stops sea level rise in this century' means even with strong mitigation, ocean thermal expansion and ice-sheet response continue due to system inertia. 'Committed to rise for centuries' indicates that sea level is a long-term commitment, with multi-meter potential depending on emissions, as grounded in paleoclimate and process models. The rate of rise — roughly doubled since the 1990s — already increases coastal flood frequency, even without stronger storms. Oppenheimer warned that planning based on historical flood maps underestimates future exposure for islands, deltas, and low-lying cities. For positive and negative feedback loops within climate systems, feedback mechanisms and monitoring, the analysis must shift from preventing rise to managing inevitable rise through elevation, protection, accommodation, and managed retreat, with timelines spanning decades to centuries.

## 3.4 Tipping Points and Abrupt Change

As Cynthia Rosenzweig (2020) argues, the potential risk of multi-breadbasket failure is increasing — food systems face escalating extremes that demand systemic adaptation. This insight anchors the analysis of tipping points and abrupt change within climate systems, feedback mechanisms and monitoring, aligning with broader evidence.

'Potential risk of multi-breadbasket failure is increasing' refers to the growing likelihood that major grain regions — North America, Europe, Asia — suffer simultaneous yield shocks in the same year. The drivers are overlapping heat, drought, and extreme rainfall made more likely by warming, combined with tightly coupled markets. 'Food systems face escalating extremes that demand systemic adaptation' means incremental farm-level tweaks alone are insufficient; supply chains, storage, trade, and diets must adapt together. Her work shows wheat, maize, and rice yields decline with each degree of warming, with larger regional variability. For tipping points and abrupt change within climate systems, feedback mechanisms and monitoring, this frames food and agriculture as systemic risk requiring diversification, resilient varieties, ecosystem-based practices, and early action on storage and distribution.

Economic considerations for tipping points and abrupt change in the context of climate systems, feedback mechanisms and monitoring show that proactive investment generally costs less than bearing escalating losses (World Bank, 2022; IPCC, 2022). Direct damages from tipping points and abrupt change to productivity and infrastructure spill over into supply chains and insurance markets. Agriculture and coastal assets exposed to tipping points and abrupt change are particularly vulnerable. Finance gaps for tipping points and abrupt change remain largest where needs are greatest, pointing to transparent risk disclosure and blended funding that reaches vulnerable groups. Cost-benefit analysis of tipping points and abrupt change therefore needs to count avoided losses, not only upfront spending. Pathways forward for tipping points and abrupt change within climate systems, feedback mechanisms and monitoring depend on pairing near-term actions with longer-term transformation (IPCC, 2023). ## 3.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Climate Systems, Feedbacks and Monitoring

For climate systems and feedbacks, the near term is about thresholds that can switch: by 2040 permafrost thaw releases 10–20% more methane, Amazon dieback risk rises where deforestation passes 20–25%, and the Atlantic overturning circulation slows 15–25%, while early-warning sensors in 30% of vulnerable basins could still catch tipping signals.

By 2050 several feedbacks begin to reinforce each other — fire-driven forest loss, soil carbon release and weaker carbon sinks — and by 2100 a high-emissions path risks passing tipping points that are effectively irreversible on human timescales (IPCC, 2022; Steffen et al., 2018).

The response is monitoring plus precaution: deploy permafrost and forest sensors, link community rain gauges into national networks, and set trigger points that automatically escalate policy when a threshold is approached.

[[TABLE:Table 3.1 Future scenarios and predictions for climate systems, feedback mechanisms and monitoring (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | methane +10–20%; AMOC −15–25% |
| 2050 | feedbacks reinforce; sinks weaken |
| 2100 | tipping risk rises sharply (high emissions) |

Watch permafrost borehole temperatures, the Amazon's dry-season greenness, and the strength of the Atlantic overturning; these indicators carry medium confidence, and their interactions are the least certain part of the picture (IPCC, 2021).

## 4.1 Heat Wave Definition and Trends

As Diana Liverman (2020) argues, climate justice means accounting for who is most responsible, who is most vulnerable, and that those least responsible for emissions bear the greatest burdens. This insight anchors the analysis of heat wave definition and trends within extreme heat, heat waves and urban heat islands, aligning with broader evidence.

'Accounting for who is most responsible, who is most vulnerable' calls for attributing emissions historically and geographically, not just current national totals. 'Those least responsible bear the greatest burdens' points to smallholder farmers, small islands, and low-income urban communities facing the earliest and harshest impacts despite low per-capita emissions. Liverman argues that responses must address land tenure, gender, and access to resources, not just technical fixes. ## 4.2 Physiology of Heat Stress

As Kristie Ebi (2018) argues, if you have cancer, you need the doctor to tell you how serious it is — people are suffering and dying right now; risks rise without urgent investment in health systems. This insight anchors the analysis of physiology of heat stress within extreme heat, heat waves and urban heat islands, aligning with broader evidence.

'If you have cancer, you need the doctor to tell you how serious it is' is her analogy for clear risk communication — understating severity delays life-saving action. 'Risks rise without urgent investment in health systems' points to the need for heat early warning, expanded primary care, and climate-resilient facilities. Ebi stresses that health evidence is actionable and economically justified, with co-benefits for productivity and equity. ## 4.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Extreme Heat and Urban Health

For extreme heat and urban health, the near term is counted in dangerous days: by 2040 days above 35 °C double to 60–90 a year in Delhi, Phoenix, Jakarta and Lagos, nights above 28 °C triple, and heat-stroke emergency visits rise 30–50%, while dense kampung neighborhoods run 5–7 °C hotter than nearby parks.

By 2050, with 0.8–1.0 °C more warming, heat becomes the leading climate killer in many cities and cuts outdoor work hours by 10–15%; by 2100 a high-emissions path adds 20–30 dangerous days a year and makes night-time cooling rare in tropical megacities (IPCC, 2022; Watts et al., 2021).

Cool roofs, street trees every 10 m, night cooling shelters, shifted work hours (06–10 and 16–19) and hydration points with heat-alert SMS are the cheapest first line of defense.

[[TABLE:Table 4.1 Future scenarios and predictions for extreme heat, heat waves and urban heat islands (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | 35 °C days +30–50%; ER visits +30% |
| 2050 | 60–90 hot days; work hours −10–15% |
| 2100 | +20–30 dangerous days; nights rarely cool |

Watch the count of nights above 28 °C, ambulance calls during heat alerts, and the temperature gap between your block and the nearest park; the heat trend is high-confidence, but neighborhood-level exposure depends on housing and shade.

## 5.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Water Scarcity and Drought

For water scarcity and drought, the near term shows up in wells and reservoirs: by 2040 renewable water per person falls 25–35% in Java, northern India and the Sahel, groundwater drops 0.5–1.5 m a year, and reservoirs deliver 30% less in dry seasons.

By 2050, drier soils and shorter rainy seasons push 1.5–2.5 billion people into water stress, and by 2100 a high-emissions path cuts river flows 30–50% and dries 40% of shallow wells (IPCC, 2022; WHO & UNICEF, 2023).

Rainwater harvesting on every roof, drip irrigation that cuts use 30–50%, managed aquifer recharge, and village water banks with clear rationing rules buy time.

[[TABLE:Table 5.1 Future scenarios and predictions for water scarcity and drought (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | renewable water −15–25% |
| 2050 | 1,5–2,5 billion in water stress |
| 2100 | river flows −30–50%; 40% wells dry |

Watch dry-season reservoir levels, the depth to groundwater in your nearest monitoring well, and the number of tanker deliveries; rainfall trends are medium-confidence, but groundwater decline is directly measurable.

## 6.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Floods and Sea Level Rise

For floods and sea-level rise, the near term rewrites return periods: by 2040 a 1-in-100-year flood becomes 1-in-20 in Jakarta Bay, the Mekong and Mississippi deltas, levees are overtopped 2–3 times more often, and tidal flooding reaches 30–50 days a year in zones below 2 m.

By 2050, 0.2–0.3 m of sea-level rise combines with heavier rainfall to make compound floods routine, and by 2100 a 0.6–1.0 m rise permanently claims 10–15% of coastal farmland and forces retreat from the lowest neighborhoods (IPCC, 2021; Oppenheimer et al., 2019).

Elevated roads and houses, mangrove belts 500 m wide, polders with reliable pumps, and buy-out or relocation offers for zones below 1 m, paired with livelihood support.

[[TABLE:Table 6.1 Future scenarios and predictions for floods and sea level rise (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | 1-in-100 → 1-in-30 flood; +0.10 m sea |
| 2050 | 1-in-100 → 1-in-15; 20–30 tidal days |
| 2100 | 0.6–1.0 m; 10–15% coastal farmland lost |

Watch annual tidal-flood days, the highest monthly tide against your street level, and drainage overflow after ordinary storms; sea level is high-confidence, but local flooding depends on subsidence and pumps (IPCC, 2022).

## 7.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Food Security and Agriculture

For food security and agriculture, the near term appears in yields and prices: by 2040 wheat yields fall 8–15% and maize 10–18% in South Asia and the Sahel, rice drops 5–10% from night heat, and tropical fish catch falls 10–20%, pushing 40–60 million more people into hunger.

By 2050, simultaneous breadbasket failures become plausible and food prices swing 20–35% in bad years; by 2100 a high-emissions path cuts staple output 20–30% without adaptation, while irrigation demand rises 30–40% (Zhao et al., 2017; FAO, 2023).

Heat-tolerant seeds, sowing 2–3 weeks earlier, drip and mulch saving 30% of water, fish ponds and village grain banks smooth the lean months.

[[TABLE:Table 7.1 Future scenarios and predictions for food security and agriculture under climate change (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | wheat −5%; maize −8% in hot zones |
| 2050 | price swings 20–35%; 40–60 m hungry |
| 2100 | staples −20–30% without adaptation |

Watch local yields per hectare, the date of the first reliable rain, and staple prices in the nearest market; crop response to heat is high-confidence, but farm-level losses depend on soil, seed choice and irrigation.

## 8.1 Biodiversity and Ecosystem Services

As Scott Kulp &  Benjamin Strauss (2019) argues, three times more people live below future high tide than old models showed — coastal risk was underestimated. This insight anchors the analysis of biodiversity and ecosystem services within ecosystems and biodiversity loss, aligning with broader evidence.

The study controls for non-climatic drivers to isolate the climate contribution. It shows coastal risk amplifies existing vulnerabilities for elevation rather than acting alone. The authors note that local Institutions can dampen or worsen the effect. Follow-up work extends the finding to new regions. ## 8.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Ecosystems and Biodiversity Loss

For ecosystems and biodiversity, the near term is measured in bleaching and range shifts: by 2040 coral reefs face annual bleaching across 90% of tropical reefs, 15–25% of bird ranges move upslope, and mangroves lose 10–20% where seas rise fastest.

By 2050, one in ten species faces a high risk of extinction at 2 °C, and by 2100 narrow-range species face 2–3 times the extinction risk under high emissions; ecosystem services that shield coasts and pollinate crops weaken in step (IPCC, 2022; IPBES, 2019).

Marine protected networks, corridors linking forest patches, replanting diverse native species, and community ranger patrols keep ecosystems connected enough to adapt.

[[TABLE:Table 8.1 Future scenarios and predictions for ecosystems and biodiversity loss (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | bleaching hits 50–70% of reefs |
| 2050 | 1 in 10 species at high extinction risk |
| 2100 | narrow-range species risk ×2–3 |

Watch coral bleaching alerts, the arrival dates of migratory birds, and mangrove cover in your district; global reef and range trends are high-confidence, but local species losses vary with habitat fragmentation.

## 9.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Health Impacts of Climate Change

For health impacts, the near term shows up in clinics: by 2040 heat deaths rise 30–50% in cities above 35 °C, dengue expands 150–200 km poleward, and diarrheal cases jump 10–20% after floods.

By 2050, heat and vector-borne disease stretch health systems thin, with 500,000+ heat deaths a year already recorded; by 2100 highland malaria doubles under high emissions without control, and mental-health burden grows alongside physical illness (Watts et al., 2021; WHO, 2023).

Heat-health alerts, screened housing, oral rehydration stocks, and clinics with flood-proof power and records protect the most exposed first.

[[TABLE:Table 9.1 Future scenarios and predictions for health impacts of climate change (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | heat deaths +20–30%; dengue +100 km |
| 2050 | 500k+ heat deaths/yr; systems strained |
| 2100 | highland malaria ×2; mental burden up |

Watch heat-related admissions, dengue case counts, and clinic closures after storms; the direction of health risk is high-confidence, but local caseloads depend on sanitation, housing and health-system capacity.

## 10.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Economic Impacts and Financial Resilience

For economic impacts and financial resilience, the near term is visible in budgets: by 2040 climate disasters cost 2–4% of GDP a year in exposed provinces, coastal insurance premiums rise 30–60%, and heat cuts work hours 10–15%.

By 2050, loss and damage claims and adaptation costs together reach hundreds of billions a year, and by 2100 a high-emissions path shaves 15–25% off incomes in vulnerable deltas compared with a low-emissions path (IPCC, 2022; World Bank, 2022).

Emergency funds equal to three months of expenses, micro-insurance pools, diversified incomes, and building codes for wind, flood and heat reduce the shock.

| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|

| 2100 | incomes −15–25% in vulnerable deltas |

## 11.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Migration and Displacement

For migration and displacement, the near term is counted in people on the move: by 2040, 15–25 million a year are displaced by floods, storms and drought, and cities such as Dhaka, Lagos and Manila absorb 300,000–500,000 climate newcomers annually.

By 2050, slow-onset loss of farmland and coastal land adds to sudden displacement, and by 2100 a high-emissions path displaces 100–150 million cumulatively from sub-3 m coasts and drying farmland (UNHCR, 2023; IPCC, 2022).

Legal aid, portable IDs and health records, rental support, and receiving-city plans for water, jobs and schools turn displacement from crisis into managed transition.

[[TABLE:Table 11.1 Future scenarios and predictions for climate migration and displacement (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | 15–20 m displaced/yr; +300k city arrivals |
| 2050 | slow-onset adds to sudden moves |
| 2100 | 100–150 m cumulative (high path) |

Watch arrival numbers in your city, shelter occupancy, and school enrollment of newcomer children; displacement totals are medium-confidence, but local inflows are recorded and can guide planning.

## 12.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Conflict, Security and Stability

For conflict and security, the near term appears where rains fail repeatedly: by 2040 competition over water and grazing drives 20–30% more local disputes in the Sahel and Horn when drought lasts 2–3 years, and storm-driven outages add to civil unrest.

By 2050, food-price spikes and displacement pressure fragile states, and by 2100 a high-emissions path widens security gaps where institutions are already weak (IPCC, 2022; Mach et al., 2019).

Community water-peace committees, early-warning SMS, shared stockpiles, and transparent governance that shares resources before scarcity peaks reduce the trigger.

[[TABLE:Table 12.1 Future scenarios and predictions for climate change, conflict and security (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | local disputes +10–15% in dry years |
| 2050 | food-price spikes; fragile states stressed |
| 2100 | security gaps widen (high path) |

Watch local water-point conflicts, food-price protests, and displacement inflows across borders; climate's link to conflict is context-dependent and medium-confidence, strongest where governance is weak.

## 13.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Infrastructure, Shelter and Energy Resilience

For infrastructure, shelter and energy, the near term is physical: by 2040 heat buckles 10–15% more roads and rails per heatwave, blackouts rise 30% when air-conditioning demand meets low water for hydropower, and 1-in-20 floods overtop drains.

By 2050, aging grids and housing face combined heat, flood and wind stress, and by 2100 a high-emissions path requires new coastal buildings to be elevated at least 1 m and redesigned for 45 °C days (IPCC, 2022; IEA, 2023).

Heat-resistant asphalt, elevated substations, solar-plus-battery mini-grids, and flood-resilient schools that double as shelters keep services running.

[[TABLE:Table 13.1 Future scenarios and predictions for infrastructure, shelter and energy resilience (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | outages +20%; road damage +10% |
| 2050 | combined heat-flood-wind stress |
| 2100 | +1 m elevation; 45 °C design days |

Watch outage hours per season, road buckling reports, and the flood level at your nearest substation; infrastructure failure thresholds are high-confidence, but actual outages depend on maintenance and backup capacity.

## 14.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Climate Literacy and Education

For climate literacy and education, the near term is measured in understanding: by 2040 misinformation spikes during every disaster, yet schools teaching climate literacy record 30–40% better preparedness scores, while 25% of adults cannot read a risk map.

By 2050, workers in heat-exposed sectors need retraining as jobs shift, and by 2100 gaps widen where curricula ignore local hazards; literacy becomes as practical as reading a tide table (IPCC, 2022; UNESCO, 2020).

Plain-language risk maps, student citizen science, teacher training, and village noticeboards with verified forecasts turn knowledge into action.

[[TABLE:Table 14.1 Future scenarios and predictions for climate literacy and education (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | preparedness +30–40% in literate schools |
| 2050 | retraining for heat-exposed jobs |
| 2100 | gaps widen where curricula ignore hazards |

Watch school preparedness drills, the share of adults who can interpret a flood map, and how fast rumors are corrected after a disaster; literacy outcomes are measurable, though attribution to any single lesson is weak.

## 15.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Data, Statistics and Misinformation

By 2050, AI-generated false imagery and cherry-picked baselines make verification a daily skill, and by 2100 poor data trust costs 10–20% extra in misdirected spending where decisions ignore uncertainty (IPCC, 2021; WMO, 2023).

## 16.1 Local Hazard Mapping and Personal Risk Matrix

As James Hansen (2013) argues, sea level rise of several meters could be expected with 2°C warming — 2°C would have consequences that can be described as disastrous. This insight anchors the analysis of local hazard mapping and personal risk matrix within household preparedness for water, food, shelter and local risk, aligning with broader evidence.

The phrase 'several meters' refers to paleoclimate evidence where past periods about 1-2°C warmer than preindustrial left sea levels 6-9 meters higher. The mechanism is not a slow linear creep but non-linear disintegration of the West Antarctic and Greenland ice sheets, amplified by ocean warming from below. The threshold of 2°C matters because it is widely presented as the upper Paris Agreement limit, often implying relative safety. Hansen argues this framing underestimates committed, century-scale risks that continue long after emissions peak. The word 'disastrous' captures his view that multi-meter rise would inundate major coastal cities, deltas, and displace hundreds of millions, with cascading economic and security effects. For local hazard mapping and personal risk matrix within household preparedness for water, food, shelter and local risk, the implication is that near-term temperature targets must be judged against long-term, irreversible sea-level commitments, not just mid-century warming levels.

## 16.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Household Preparedness — Water, Food, Shelter and Risk

For household preparedness, the near term is practical: by 2040 households without three days of water and food face 2–3 times more crisis visits, homes below 2 m flood yearly, and kitchens fail 30% more often in heat.

By 2050, insurance and aid arrive slower than neighbors, and by 2100 a high-emissions path requires rain tanks and cool rooms in half of all homes (IFRC, 2022; WHO, 2023).

Go-bags, 15 liters of water per person per day for three days, grain banks, a cool shelter, and a neighborhood mutual-aid roster cover the first 72 hours.

[[TABLE:Table 16.1 Future scenarios and predictions for household preparedness for water, food, shelter and local risk (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | 3-day kit cuts losses 20–30% |
| 2050 | aid lags; neighbors first responders |
| 2100 | rain tanks/cool rooms in 50% of homes |

Watch your stored water days, the flood mark on your street, and how long the last outage lasted; household readiness is directly measurable and cheap to improve.

## 17.1 First Aid Basics and Heat Illness

As Johan Rockström (2009) argues, we define planetary boundaries within which humanity can operate safely — six of nine boundaries are now transgressed, well outside the safe operating space. This insight anchors the analysis of first aid basics and heat illness within health, emergency response and protecting vulnerable populations, aligning with broader evidence.

'Planetary boundaries' are quantitative thresholds for climate, biosphere, freshwater, nutrients, and other Earth-system processes that define a Holocene-like stability zone. 'Operate safely' means staying within these thresholds reduces risk of abrupt, irreversible shifts. 'Six of nine transgressed' indicates humanity has left the safe zone on climate, biodiversity, land, freshwater, biogeochemical flows, and novel entities, far outside preindustrial variability. The update emphasizes interactions — transgressing one boundary tightens others, such as deforestation amplifying carbon release. The concept reframes first aid basics and heat illness from isolated sectoral problem to systemic interdependence. For health, emergency response and protecting vulnerable populations, it implies solutions must be integrated across energy, food, and ecosystem management rather than siloed.

## 17.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Health, Emergency Response and Special Populations

For emergency response and vulnerable populations, the near term is counted in minutes: by 2040 ambulance delays rise 20% during heat and floods, clinics lose power 10–15% of storm days, and mortality among older and disabled people runs 2–3 times higher without outreach.

By 2050, simultaneous heat, flood and disease events stretch responders beyond capacity, and by 2100 a high-emissions path overwhelms formal services without community-based teams (WHO, 2023; IFRC, 2022).

Volunteer responder teams, door-to-door registries of vulnerable residents, clinic backup power, and mobile health units close the gap in the first hours.

[[TABLE:Table 17.1 Future scenarios and predictions for health, emergency response and protecting vulnerable populations (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | ambulance delays +20%; outages 10–15% |
| 2050 | compound events exceed capacity |
| 2100 | formal services overwhelmed without volunteers |

Watch response times, the number of vulnerable residents with a named contact, and clinic backup-power hours; response capacity is testable through drills, not guesswork.

## 18.3 Community Leaders and Inclusive Participation

As Michael Oppenheimer (2020) argues, there is no scenario that stops sea level rise in this century — sea level is committed to rise for centuries due to ocean heat and ice-sheet response. This insight anchors the analysis of community leaders and inclusive participation within social capital, mutual aid and community governance, aligning with broader evidence.

'No scenario stops sea level rise in this century' means even with strong mitigation, ocean thermal expansion and ice-sheet response continue due to system inertia. 'Committed to rise for centuries' indicates that sea level is a long-term commitment, with multi-meter potential depending on emissions, as grounded in paleoclimate and process models. The rate of rise — roughly doubled since the 1990s — already increases coastal flood frequency, even without stronger storms. Oppenheimer warned that planning based on historical flood maps underestimates future exposure for islands, deltas, and low-lying cities. ## 18.4 Principles of Mutual Aid and Starting a Group

'Potential risk of multi-breadbasket failure is increasing' refers to the growing likelihood that major grain regions — North America, Europe, Asia — suffer simultaneous yield shocks in the same year. The drivers are overlapping heat, drought, and extreme rainfall made more likely by warming, combined with tightly coupled markets. 'Food systems face escalating extremes that demand systemic adaptation' means incremental farm-level tweaks alone are insufficient; supply chains, storage, trade, and diets must adapt together. Her work shows wheat, maize, and rice yields decline with each degree of warming, with larger regional variability. ## 18.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Social Capital, Mutual Aid and Governance

For social capital and mutual aid, the near term is relational: by 2040 neighborhoods with strong mutual aid recover 30–40% faster and report 20% fewer losses, while fragmented areas wait one to two weeks longer for help.

By 2050, trust becomes a form of infrastructure as repeated disasters exhaust formal systems, and by 2100 a high-emissions path widens the gap between connected and isolated communities (Aldrich & Meyer, 2015; IPCC, 2022).

Block captains, tool libraries, community funds, and joint decisions on water and evacuation turn neighbors into a first-response network.

[[TABLE:Table 18.1 Future scenarios and predictions for social capital, mutual aid and community governance (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | recovery 30–40% faster with strong ties |
| 2050 | trust as infrastructure; repeated shocks |
| 2100 | connected vs isolated gap widens |

Watch how many households know their block captain, how quickly a tool library responds, and whether decisions are shared; social cohesion is measurable through simple surveys and drills.

## 19.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Renewable Energy and Appropriate Technologies

For renewable energy and appropriate technologies, the near term is economic: by 2040 solar-plus-storage is cheaper than diesel in 70% of off-grid areas, but heat cuts panel output 5–10% and storms damage 15% of exposed installations.

By 2050, mini-grids and battery cooperatives become the backbone of resilient power, and by 2100 a high-emissions path still leaves 20% of demand on fossil fuels without storage scale-up (IEA, 2023; IPCC, 2022).

## 21.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Governance, Justice and Civic Engagement

For governance, justice and civic engagement, the near term is budgetary: by 2040 climate lawsuits double while adaptation budgets meet only 15–25% of needs, and low-income wards receive 30% less protection.

By 2050, unequal exposure becomes a political fault line, and by 2100 a high-emissions path locks in injustice where decisions are made without community voice (IPCC, 2022; Schlosberg, 2007).

Participatory budgeting, equity audits, legal aid, and public transparency boards that track spending put resources where risk is highest.

[[TABLE:Table 21.1 Future scenarios and predictions for climate governance, justice, equity and civic engagement (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | adaptation budgets 15–25% of needs |
| 2050 | unequal exposure becomes political |
| 2100 | injustice locked in without voice |

Watch per-capita adaptation spending by ward, the number of community consultations, and lawsuit outcomes; distributional data are available and can be audited.

## 22.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Case Studies — Heat, Fire, Flood and Urban Extremes

For cities facing heat, fire, flood and urban extremes, the near term is already documented: by 2040 Ahmedabad, Los Angeles and Athens see 30–50% more wildfire-smoke days, and low-lying urban zones face 40–60 flood days a year.

By 2050, 1-in-10-year urban floods become 1-in-5 in many deltas, and by 2100 a high-emissions path makes today's 1-in-10-year flood an annual event in the most exposed cities (IPCC, 2022; UNDRR, 2015).

Fire breaks, sponge-city parks, early evacuation drills, and city-to-city learning exchanges spread what already works.

[[TABLE:Table 22.1 Future scenarios and predictions for regional case studies of heat, wildfire, flood and urban climate extremes (2030-2100)]]
| Horizon | What changes for this chapter |
|---|---|
| 2030 | smoke days +30–40%; flood days +20 |
| 2050 | 1-in-10 urban flood → 1-in-5 |
| 2100 | 1-in-10 flood becomes annual |

Watch smoke-day counts, flood days in your district, and evacuation times in the last drill; city-level records are strong, though each city's exposure differs.

## 23.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Case Studies — Drought, Food, Water and Security

For drought, food, water and security hotspots, the near term repeats recent crises: by 2040 Horn of Africa and Sahel droughts like 2022 recur every 3–4 years, Mekong dry-season flows fall 20%, and Punjab groundwater drops 1 m a year, pushing food prices up 20–35% in bad years.

By 2050, multi-breadbasket failures become more likely, and by 2100 a high-emissions path doubles the risk of simultaneous crop losses across continents (FAO, 2023; IPCC, 2022).

Drought-tolerant crops, watershed recharge, grain reserves, and cross-border water agreements buffer the shock before it becomes a famine.

## 24.6 Future Scenarios and Predictions: Action Plans and Checklists for Survival

For personal and community action plans, the near term is about readiness: by 2040 families with a one-month plan cut losses 40–50% and recover twice as fast, while 30% without checklists forget documents and medicines in an evacuation.

# Appendix B: Glosarium

- Adaptation: Adjusting to actual or expected climate impacts.
- Mitigation: Reducing greenhouse gas emissions.
- Resilience: Capacity to absorb disturbance and reorganize.
- Vulnerability: Susceptibility to harm.
- Exposure: Presence of people or assets in hazard-prone areas.
- Sensitivity: Degree to which a system is affected by climate.
- Adaptive capacity: Ability to adjust to climate change.
- Tipping point: Threshold beyond which change becomes self-sustaining.
- Greenhouse effect: Trapping of heat by atmospheric gases.
- Carbon footprint: Total greenhouse gas emissions caused by an entity.
- SSP: Shared Socioeconomic Pathway (scenario framework).
- RCP: Representative Concentration Pathway.
- Committed warming: Warming locked in by past emissions.

# Appendix E: Biografi Penulis

**Setyo Wisodo** adalah profesional yang menggabungkan keahlian dalam Pengembangan AI, Manajemen Produk, dan Pemasaran Digital. Aktif dalam pengembangan solusi berbasis AI dan produk digital. Lokasi: Indonesia.

# Appendix F: Extended Analytical Notes

Analytical note: The relationship between climate drivers and social outcomes is mediated by institutional capacity, resource availability, and historical context. No single factor determines the result.

Analytical note: Adaptation strategies must be evaluated not only for effectiveness but also for equity, cost, and feasibility in specific contexts.

Analytical note: The temporal dimension of climate impacts requires long-term thinking that often conflicts with short-term political and economic incentives.

Analytical note: Knowledge production and knowledge use are distinct processes; bridging them requires deliberate effort and sustained engagement.

Analytical note: Uncertainty is inherent in climate science and should be communicated transparently rather than hidden or exaggerated.
